● I've reviewed the spec and your machine setup, and nothing in the environment
blocks the project—the main risks are getting the DQN to learn efficiently
from long rounds and late rewards, calibrating win probabilities, and handling
hidden info in a stateless backend. I'll start asking clarifying questions in
rounds, beginning with core rules.

✻ Baked for 18s · done Sunday 8:33 PM
Turn 3

gemini vs groq vs openai

Claude's response · 70 lines 
● Here's how they compare for this project, given the $0 budget:

Cost
Groq: Free tier, no card needed
Gemini (Google AI Studio): Free tier, no card needed
OpenAI: No standing free tier. You pay per token after adding credit, which
breaks the $0 rule.
────────────────────────────────────────
Speed
Groq: Fastest by far (hundreds of tokens/s). An explanation comes back in
under
a second.
Gemini (Google AI Studio): Good, usually 1–3 s
OpenAI: Good
────────────────────────────────────────
Models
Groq: Open models (Llama, Qwen, etc.)
Gemini (Google AI Studio): Gemini Flash models, which are strong at reasoning
OpenAI: GPT models, strongest overall
────────────────────────────────────────
Free-tier limits
Groq: Per-minute and per-day request caps, plenty for a demo
Gemini (Google AI Studio): Per-minute and per-day caps, also plenty for a
demo;
free-tier data may be used by Google to improve its products
OpenAI: n/a
────────────────────────────────────────
Explanation quality
Groq: Good. The text only has to turn facts we compute into English.
Gemini (Google AI Studio): Good to very good
OpenAI: Very good
────────────────────────────────────────
Risk
Groq: Rate limits during heavy testing
Gemini (Google AI Studio): Free-tier terms change fairly often
OpenAI: Costs money

The LLM won't be doing the analysis. The engine and model compute the facts:
which melds you're close to, what each discard is worth, the win probability.
The LLM only turns those facts into a sentence or two. Any of the three does
that well, so speed and cost decide it, and quality barely matters.

My recommendation: Groq as the main provider, because a tutor panel that
answers instantly feels real-time. Gemini sits behind the same interface as a
backup, chosen by an environment variable. If neither key is set or a limit is
hit, the panel falls back to template text. OpenAI can be plugged into the
same interface later if you ever have credits, but it won't be the default.
Free-tier limits change often, so I'll treat the exact numbers as unknown and
build in caching and rate-limit handling.

Here are the remaining questions again:

Claude's response · 30 lines 
● Understood, I'll decide both:

- Game state: the server keeps each game in memory under a random game ID. The
  browser only ever sees what that player is allowed to see, so the deck
  can't be peeked at in devtools. Refreshing the page starts a new game, and
  abandoned games expire automatically. No accounts or saved progress.
- Reveal: when you can form an original rummy, the UI highlights your options
  and pre-selects the coach's best choice. You confirm it or pick a different
  one, which teaches the rule while you play.

A last round about the Learn tab and the coaching panel:

Wrote 3 memories

● I've written SPEC.md (the rules and technical spec) and plan.md (the phased
build plan) in the project folder. It's buildable by Tuesday 10:00 IST. The
main risk is whether the trained AI ends up stronger than a hand-written bot,
and the plan works either way.

Feasibility, in short
- Full 156-card / 13-card game kept. The game's size isn't the bottleneck.
  What matters is how fast the engine can work out the best way to group a
  hand into rummies, and that doesn't depend on deck size. The 10- and 7-card
  versions are a config switch if ever needed.
- Training is the risky part. To make it likely to work in one night:
  - The engine handles grouping, revealing and declaring automatically, so the
    AI only has to learn which pile to draw from, what to discard and when to
    forfeit.
  - It gets small rewards along the way, not just at the end of a round.
  - It trains first against a decent rule-based bot.
- If the AI is still weaker than that rule-based bot, Hard mode uses whichever
  is stronger. Either way, the results file records both honestly.
- Win % comes from a separate small model trained on game outcomes. The AI's
  raw move scores aren't real probabilities, so they're only shown as "AI
  preference".
- Hosting: GitHub Pages can only serve static files, so the website goes there
  and the Python backend runs on a free Hugging Face Space. That also keeps
  the Gemini key secret.

Rules from our Q&A are now in SPEC §3, each with an ID that the tests will
reference. Calls I made that you didn't explicitly confirm are listed in SPEC
§12 (A1–A10), so they're easy to check and change. The ones most worth a look:
- A1: revealing your original is forced at the end of the turn you form it.
- A2: after taking the open card, you also can't discard an identical copy of
  it.
- A5: a round ends with no winner after 300 turns (a safety net that should
  never trigger).
- A8: equal totals at the end of 7 rounds are a draw.

Plan outline:
- Engine tonight.
- Training starts around 03:30 and runs while you sleep.
- Monday: the backend and website, built against the rule-based bot so they
  never wait for the model. The trained model is swapped in once it's
  evaluated.
- Tuesday: testing on the live site, then freeze at 08:30.
- If time runs short, plan.md has an ordered list of what to cut, and a list
  of things that are never cut.

What you do now (Phase 0 in plan.md):
1. 0A, about 20 min, before anything else: set up the Python environment, run
   the RLCard check, and set the laptop to stay awake on charger. Pausing
   Windows Update matters most, since an overnight reboot would kill training.
2. 0B, about 40 min, while I build the engine: create the GitHub, Hugging Face
   and Google AI Studio accounts and add the keys where the steps say.
3. Then send me your GitHub username, your Hugging Face username and the
   Gemini model name (no keys needed).

Once 0A passes, start Phase 1 with: "Read SPEC.md and plan.md. Implement Phase
1."

● Found the bug: round 4 had the bots stuck in a loop taking each other's
discards 267 times in 300 turns. I'll fix this by tracking each player's
discard history (which is public info anyway) and preventing a bot from
reclaiming a card it previously discarded, unless doing so wins the round.

Ran 3 shell commands

● Found it: the bot was picking 0-point discards that didn't actually complete
the exact 3/3/3/4 win shape, causing endless cycling. I'll fix it by adding an
exact win check to every 0-point discard, penalizing leftover unmelded cards,
and giving the observation a per-discard "wins" flag.

● That's slower than expected: 1,000 rounds took more than 10 minutes. I'll

Read 1 file

● The slowdown comes from untrained self-play producing ~200-turn rounds versus
~14 for skilled play, making each round ~15× costlier. I'll fix this by
pretraining on bot demonstrations (DQfD), gating self-play behind a 40% win
rate, and capping rounds at 120 turns.

    222 +def pretrain(agent: QRummyDQNAgent, demos: dict, steps: int, m
        +argin: float, lam: float, gamma: float):
    223 +    """DQfD-style pretraining (Hester et al. 2018): double-DQN
        + TD loss + large-margin
    224 +    supervised loss towards the demonstrated (heuristic) actio
        +n, on RLCard's Q-network."""
    225 +    qnet, tnet = agent.q_estimator.qnet, agent.target_estimato
        +r.qnet
    226 +    opt = agent.q_estimator.optimizer
    227 +    dev = agent.device
    228 +    N, B = len(demos["a"]), agent.batch_size
    229 +    t = {k: torch.from_numpy(v) for k, v in demos.items()}
    230 +    for k in range(steps):
    231 +        idx = torch.from_numpy(np.random.randint(N, size=B))
    232 +        s = t["s"][idx].float().to(dev)
    233 +        s2 = t["s2"][idx].float().to(dev)
    234 +        a = t["a"][idx].long().to(dev)
    235 +        r = t["r"][idx].to(dev)
    236 +        d = t["d"][idx].float().to(dev)
    237 +        m = t["m"][idx].to(dev)
    238 +        m2 = t["m2"][idx].to(dev)
    239 +        with torch.no_grad():
    240 +            qnet.eval()
    241 +            best = qnet(s2).masked_fill(~m2, -1e9).argmax(1)
    242 +            target = r + gamma * (1 - d) * tnet(s2).gather(1,
        +best[:, None]).squeeze(1)
    243 +        qnet.train()
    244 +        q = qnet(s)
    245 +        qa = q.gather(1, a[:, None]).squeeze(1)
    246 +        td = torch.nn.functional.mse_loss(qa, target)
    247 +        bonus = margin * (1 - torch.nn.functional.one_hot(a, N
        +UM_ACTIONS).float())
    248 +        large_margin = ((q + bonus).masked_fill(~m, -1e9).max(
        +1).values - qa).mean()
    249 +        loss = td + lam * large_margin
    250 +        opt.zero_grad()
    251 +        loss.backward()
    252 +        opt.step()
    253 +        if k % 1000 == 0:
    254 +            tnet.load_state_dict(qnet.state_dict())
    255 +            print(f"  pretrain {k}/{steps} td {td.item():.4f}
        +margin {large_margin.item():.4f}", flush=True)
    256 +    qnet.eval()
    257 +    tnet.load_state_dict(qnet.state_dict())
    258 +

    100 +| ID | Decision | Why | By | Ref | Status |
    101 +|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    102 +| D-058 | "Outs" is an approximation: the unseen copies of car
        +d types that complete a meld from two uncovered cards, plus ev
        +ery unseen wild when at least two natural cards are uncovered.
        + Pre-original "original outs" are exact: the unseen cards that
        + complete a natural original with two cards already held | Exa
        +ct outs would need about 50 solver calls per candidate discard
        + (too slow for training). The approximation costs microseconds
        + and keeps the signal that matters. | Claude | §5.2, `features
        +.py` | Active |
    103 +| D-059 | Before RL, the DQN is **pretrained on the heuristic'
        +s games in DQfD style**: RLCard's double-DQN TD loss plus a la
        +rge-margin imitation loss (margin 0.8) on 60k heuristic transi
        +tions. Those transitions also warm-start the replay buffer | A
        +n untrained DQN plays near-randomly, and random rounds last ~2
        +00 turns against ~14 for competent play. Self-play from scratc
        +h would waste most of the night. Pretraining gives a competent
        + starting policy that RL then improves. The RLCard network and
        + update rule are unchanged (consistent with D-032). | Claude |
        + §5.5, `train_dqn.py` | Active |
    104 +| D-060 | Heuristic fixes: (a) it never takes back an open car
        +d it discarded earlier in the round, unless that card wins; (b
        +) it always plays a winning discard (exact-cover check, becaus
        +e 0 points ≠ win when wild cards are left out of melds); (c) i
        +t penalises uncovered cards | Heuristic-vs-heuristic rounds lo
        +oped until the turn cap (46%, then 31% undecided). After the f
        +ix: 100% decided, ~14 turns per round, ~10 rounds/s. | Claude
        +| §5.6, `heuristic.py` | Active |
    105 +| D-061 | The observation block I has **4 rows per type** (poi
        +nts after the discard, in best arrangement, outs, **discard wi
        +ns**), so `OBS_DIM` = **992** (was 939) | The policy must see
        +when a discard wins: low points alone don't say that (D-060b).
        + | Claude | §5.2 | Active |
    106 +| D-062 | Training settings: round cap 120 turns (training onl
        +y; real games keep 300); ε 0.15 → 0.03; self-play only once th
        +e DQN wins ≥ 40% of rounds against the heuristic; evaluations
        +during training use the same cap | Keeps the throughput high a
        +nd the self-play opponents meaningful. | Claude | §5.5 | Activ
        +e |
    107 +| D-063 | `QRummyDQNAgent` subclasses RLCard's `DQNAgent`: an
        +O(1) ring replay buffer storing float16 observations, no conso
        +le print per training step, and the target-network copy done w
        +ith `load_state_dict` instead of `deepcopy` | RLCard's list bu
        +ffer uses `pop(0)` (O(n)) and float64 arrays (~4.5 GB at 300k)

        +te maths is identical. | Claude | `train_dqn.py` | Active |

     97 +def large_margin_loss(q: torch.Tensor, a: torch.Tensor, legal:
        + torch.Tensor, margin: float) -> torch.Tensor:
     98 +    """DQfD supervised loss: max_a'[Q(s,a') + l(a_E,a')] - Q(s
        +,a_E), over legal actions."""
     99 +    qa = q.gather(1, a[:, None]).squeeze(1)
    100 +    bonus = margin * (1 - torch.nn.functional.one_hot(a, q.sha
        +pe[1]).float())
    101 +    return ((q + bonus).masked_fill(~legal, -1e9).max(1).value
        +s - qa).mean()
    102 +
    103 +
    104  class QRummyDQNAgent(DQNAgent):
    105      """RLCard DQNAgent with a ring buffer and without per-step
          console output.
     99 -    train() is RLCard's Double-DQN update, unchanged apart fro
        -m logging."""
    106
    107 +    train() is RLCard's Double-DQN update. When demonstrations
        + are attached (D-065), the same
    108 +    optimizer step also adds λ·large-margin loss on a demo bat
        +ch (DQfD); λ = 0 gives RLCard's
    109 +    update exactly."""
    110 +
    111      def __init__(self, **kw):
    112          super().__init__(**kw)
    113          self.memory = RingMemory(kw["replay_memory_size"], kw[
         "batch_size"])
    114          self.losses: list[float] = []
    115 +        self.demo: dict | None = None
    116 +        self.demo_lambda = 0.0
    117 +        self.demo_margin = 0.8
    118
    119 +    def attach_demos(self, demos: dict, margin: float):
    120 +        dev = self.device
    121 +        self.demo = {"s": torch.from_numpy(demos["s"]).to(dev)
        +, "a": torch.from_numpy(demos["a"]).long().to(dev),
    122 +                     "m": torch.from_numpy(demos["m"]).to(dev)
        +}
    123 +        self.demo_margin = margin
    124 +
    125      def train(self):
    126          state_batch, action_batch, reward_batch, next_state_ba
         tch, done_batch, legal_actions_batch = self.memory.sample()
    127          q_values_next = self.q_estimator.predict_nograd(next_s
         tate_batch)
   ...

● Extended pretraining didn't help—agreement stayed at 44% and win rate
plateaued at 33.5%, matching the 6k-step ceiling. I'll launch the overnight
run with 6k pretraining steps instead, keeping imitation loss active during RL
and tracking a hybrid model (DQN picking among the bot's top 3 discards) as
the likely Hard-mode candidate.

    106  | D-062 | Training settings: round cap 120 turns (training onl
         y; real games keep 300); ε 0.15 → 0.03; self-play only once th
         e DQN wins ≥ 40% of rounds against the heuristic; evaluations
         during training use the same cap | Keeps the throughput high a
         nd the self-play opponents meaningful. | Claude | §5.5 | Activ
         e |
    107  | D-063 | `QRummyDQNAgent` subclasses RLCard's `DQNAgent`: an
         O(1) ring replay buffer storing float16 observations, no conso
         le print per training step, and the target-network copy done w
         ith `load_state_dict` instead of `deepcopy` | RLCard's list bu
         ffer uses `pop(0)` (O(n)) and float64 arrays (~4.5 GB at 300k)
         . Its per-step prints would flood the overnight logs. The upda
         te maths is identical. | Claude | `train_dqn.py` | Active |
    108  | D-064 | `get_state` now includes every player's public disca
         rd history (`my_discards`, `opponents[].discards`) | Discards
         are public information (R11). The heuristic needs them for D-0
         60a, and the coach can use them to explain moves. | Claude | §
         4.4, R11 | Active |
    109 +| D-066 | Partly supersedes D-061. Block I gets **6 rows per t
        +ype**, adding "uncovered cards after the discard" and "an orig
        +inal remains after the discard"; `OBS_DIM` = **1098**, `FEATUR
        +E_VERSION` = 2 | Imitation probe: with 4 rows the network agre
        +ed with only 42% of the heuristic's discards; these are the tw
        +o terms the heuristic's discard score uses that the observatio
        +n didn't show. After the change: 36.5% win rate against the he
        +uristic after pretraining (was 32.5%). | Claude | §5.2 | Activ
        +e |
    110 +| D-067 | Pretraining stays at 6k steps (15k gave no gain: 44%
        + discard agreement, 33.5% win rate). Every training evaluation
        + also scores the **hybrid** (DQN choosing among the heuristic'
        +s top 3) against the heuristic | RLCard's plain MLP has a sepa
        +rate output per card type, so it learns "pick the best-scoring
        + card" one slot at a time; that, plus tied scores, caps exact
        +imitation. Changing the network would break the RLCard-DQN req
        +uirement (D-032). The hybrid is the most likely Hard policy (D
        +-034), so it is tracked from the start. | Claude | §5.5, §5.8

    113 +| D-070 | The coach's "win % after this move" evaluates hypoth

        +from the human (AI hand, draw pile, unseen CJ) is reshuffled f
        +irst; closed draws average 16 samples; positions after a disca
        +rd are recast as "you to draw next" | The value net is trained

    102  | D-058 | "Outs" is an approximation: the unseen copies of car
         d types that complete a meld from two uncovered cards, plus ev
         ery unseen wild when at least two natural cards are uncovered.
          Pre-original "original outs" are exact: the unseen cards that
          complete a natural original with two cards already held | Exa
         ct outs would need about 50 solver calls per candidate discard
          (too slow for training). The approximation costs microseconds
          and keeps the signal that matters. | Claude | §5.2, `features
         .py` | Active |
    103  | D-059 | Before RL, the DQN is **pretrained on the heuristic'
         s games in DQfD style**: RLCard's double-DQN TD loss plus a la
         rge-margin imitation loss (margin 0.8) on 60k heuristic transi
         tions. Those transitions also warm-start the replay buffer | A
         n untrained DQN plays near-randomly, and random rounds last ~2
         00 turns against ~14 for competent play. Self-play from scratc
         h would waste most of the night. Pretraining gives a competent
          starting policy that RL then improves. The RLCard network and
          update rule are unchanged (consistent with D-032). | Claude |
          §5.5, `train_dqn.py` | Active |

● The Learn tab is built: a guided practice round against Easy with the coach
always on and tips at the right moments, 60 practice puzzles, and 7 strategy
notes. Every answer and figure on it comes from the game engine, not my
writing. A headless-browser run against the local backend played a guided
round and a Play round to the end with no errors. The site build is clean, and
all 10 backend tests pass.

Bugs found and fixed by playing through the site:
- Coach recommended forfeiting: until a trained model is loaded, the coach
  ranked "Forfeit (40 pts)" first on almost every first turn. It now suggests
  forfeiting only for genuinely hopeless hands, and a test guards against the
  bug coming back.
- Reveal popup lingered: after you clicked Reveal, the popup stayed on screen
  with a greyed-out button through the AI's whole turn. It now closes straight
  away.
- Hidden tip: in the guided round, the first tip appeared behind the reveal
  popup, so you could never read it. The popup explains revealing itself, so
  tips now wait until it closes.
- Mobile layout: the end-of-round summary ran off the side of a phone screen.
  It now wraps.
- Missing pages on GitHub Pages: the current Next.js version expects some
  background-loading files at the wrong path, so they would fail on GitHub
  Pages. A small step after each build now copies them to the right place. I
  also added a site icon.

On the strategy page, the figures currently come from 3,000 games between
copies of the rule-based bot, not the trained AI. The page says so, and I'll
regenerate them with the final AI after training. Headline numbers: whoever
reveals their original first wins 67.9% of rounds; a player who never reveals
wins 7.8%.

Training run A is about 40 minutes in and healthy. After the initial imitation
step it already wins 37.5% against the rule-based bot, the best start of any
attempt so far, with no illegal moves, at about 2.6 rounds/s. It finishes
around Mon 08:20. The GPU sits mostly idle because the game simulation on the
CPU is the bottleneck; that's expected.

decisions.md has new entries D-075 to D-081, SPEC.md is at v1.4 with the Learn
section rewritten to match what was built, and plan.md checkboxes now reflect
Phases 2 to 6.

● Update(SPEC.md)
⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
    68  | DQN learning from self-play overnight | ⚠️ **Main risk** | Mi
        tigations: (1) the solver does the meld arrangement, the reveal
         choice and the declaration, so the DQN only learns draw, disca
        rd and forfeit; (2) solver-derived features in the observation;
         (3) potential-based reward shaping; (4) a curriculum against a
         strong heuristic bot plus a replay buffer warm-started from he
        uristic play; (5) the heuristic is the fallback for Hard if the
         DQN ends up weaker (§5.8). |
    69  | Win probability | ⚠️ Q ≠ probability | A separate **value net
        work** trained on self-play outcomes, with a calibration check
        (§6.1). Q-values are shown only as "AI preference". |
    70  | "Stateless" backend | ⚠️ Hidden information | **In-memory ses
        sions** keyed by an unguessable `game_id`. The client never rec
        eives hidden information. Refresh means a new game (the user's
        requirement). §7.2. |
    71 -| GitHub Pages hosting | ⚠️ Static only | The frontend is a sta
       -tic export on **GitHub Pages**. The backend runs on a **Hugging
       - Face Space** (free CPU Docker, 2 vCPU / 16 GB). It is the free
       - host that comfortably runs PyTorch and keeps the Gemini key se
       -cret. §9. |
    71 +| GitHub Pages hosting | ⚠️ Static only | The frontend is a sta

       +* free web service (512 MB / 0.1 CPU) with NumPy-only inference
       +, and keeps the Gemini key secret. Hugging Face Docker Spaces t

● The Q-values are diverging (from +0.37 to +4.84, well past the theoretical +1
return cap), which explains the drop in agreement with the rule-based

instead of 0.1—all gated behind config keys so run A stays reproducible.

● Committing the trainer change (no .md files) and arming a watch for run C's

Ran 2 shell commands

● Run B started getting worse after 10,000 rounds, so I stopped it and started a
third run, run C, with a fix for the cause. Run C runs until about Mon 09:35,
and I'm watching for its first evaluation (about 25 minutes away).

Why B got worse: in most positions only a few of the legal discards ever get
tried. The network never learns accurate scores for the rest, so some keep
inflated scores and those errors feed into its training. Capping the scores
(the fix that started B) only limited the damage.

Run C's change: it pulls down the scores of moves it hasn't tried, compared
with the move it actually played. This is a standard technique for exactly
this problem (conservative Q-learning, CQL). It also saves a snapshot every 15
minutes instead of every 30, so I can keep whichever point in the run plays
best.

Run B's best snapshot (the hybrid at 44.5% against the rule-based bot) and run
A's snapshots are kept as fallbacks. In the morning I'll pick the strongest
across all three runs.

● Good morning update, part two: the trained models are now live on the site and
everything is working.

Which AI plays each difficulty. I tested 20 saved versions of the AI from all
three overnight runs, then reran the best few over 4,000 rounds each. Win
rates against the hand-written heuristic:

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────┐
│                           Player                            │ Win rate │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
│ Heuristic vs itself (the baseline)                          │ 51.3%    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
│ Hybrid (network picks among the heuristic's top 3 discards) │ 46.9%    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┤
│ Pure network (DQN)                                          │ 35.0%    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────┘

- The overnight self-play training made the network weaker in all three runs.
  The strongest version is the one saved right after it learned by copying the
  heuristic, before any self-play. That's the one shipping.
- Hard is the heuristic itself, since our rule is "Hard = strongest player".
- Medium is the network playing on its own.
- Easy is the heuristic making random moves 35% of the time.
- The original Medium rule ("closest to 50% against Hard") would have picked
  something nearly as strong as Hard, so I changed it.
- The network still beats a random player 95.8% of the time, which passes the
  Phase 5 gate.
- Coach change: it now suggests the hybrid's move first, because the network
  on its own makes clearly worse discards.
- Site text: I rewrote it to say what each level actually is, and no longer
  claims self-play improved the AI.

Win-probability model: its first version looked fine but was badly
overfitting. I fixed how it's tested and made it keep its best training pass.
On rounds it never saw, it now predicts the winner 64.4% of the time and its
probabilities are off by about 2 percentage points on average (our limit was
5).

Live site
- All 282 tests pass.
- With the models loaded, the server got much slower: hints took 3.9 s and
  moves 2.9 s. The cause was the maths library starting a thread per core on
  Render's 0.1 CPU. I pinned it to one thread. Hints now take about 0.4 s and
  moves about 0.2 s.
- Gemini wrote 5 of the 6 explanations in the latest browser test, with no
  errors.

The local plan, spec and decision log are updated (D-096 to D-099). None of
them are in git.

Next for me: write the README, run the QA checklist on the live site, check
the AI makes zero illegal moves across 50 automated matches, and polish.

For you: the playtest can happen any time now, no need to wait for this
evening. Play a round or two on each difficulty at
https://coder-r2.github.io/qrummy/play/ with the coach on, and tell me
anything that feels off.

● Phase 7 is done, and everything is pushed and live. That puts us about 21
hours ahead of the Tuesday 10:00 deadline.

- Illegal-move check: I added a test that plays 50 full matches against all
  three difficulties, with a "human" making random legal moves. The AI never
  once made an illegal move.
- Data formats: I checked every field the backend sends against the website's
  type definitions. The only gap was one unused duplicate field, now added to
  the types.
- QA checklist on the live site: a headless browser run passes all 32 checks:
  - Every page loads with no errors, and none use "meld" or "deadwood".
  - The coach is off by default, a new round deals 13 cards, and forfeit is
    offered only on the first turn.
  - Refreshing returns to the lobby, and the guided round starts and shows a
    tip.
  - All 60 puzzles can be answered, and the strategy notes show their numbers.
  - Phones in portrait and landscape have no sideways scrolling.

  The rule details themselves (scoring, the 80-point cap, reshuffles) are
  covered by the engine's tests rather than clicked through.
- Polish: animations now respect your device's "reduce motion" setting. The
  pauses between AI moves stay, so you can still follow what it did.
- README: what the project is, links, an architecture diagram, how to run,
  test and train it, and how it's deployed.
  - It's upfront that self-play didn't beat imitation, so Hard is the
    heuristic and the network plays Medium.
  - There's no licence file, so the README doesn't mention one. Tell me if you
    want one added (MIT is the usual choice).
